

# Cyber Risk Institute (CRI) Profile v2

This AWS Config managed conformance pack provides a curated mapping between the Cyber Risk Institute (CRI) Profile Version 2 diagnostic statements and AWS Config managed rules. Each Config rule evaluates specific AWS resources and is associated with one or more CRI Profile diagnostic statements. AWS Config maintains and automatically updates the managed conformance pack as supported rules and resource types evolve. Refer to the table below for details on the rules, associated controls, guidance, and AWS Region availability.

**Source identifier:** CRI-PROFILE\_V2

This conformance pack is available in all commercial AWS Regions (Regions other than the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions and the China Regions). It's also available in AWS GovCloud (US) Regions.

This conformance pack contains up to 723 AWS Config managed rules. The rules deployed vary by Region. The following table lists each rule, its control IDs, guidance, and the Regions where it's available. To see details about a rule, choose its name.



| AWS Config Rule | Control IDs | Guidance | Region availability | 
| --- | --- | --- | --- | 
| [access-keys-rotated](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/access-keys-rotated.html) | PR.AA-01.01, PR.AA-05.03 | Rotating access keys will reduce the window of opportunity for an access key that is associated with a compromised or terminated account to be used. Access keys should be rotated to ensure that data cannot be accessed with an old key which might have been lost, cracked, or stolen. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Hyderabad), Asia Pacific (Malaysia), Asia Pacific (Melbourne), Asia Pacific (New Zealand), Asia Pacific (Taipei), Asia Pacific (Thailand), Canada West (Calgary), Europe (Spain), Europe (Zurich), Israel (Tel Aviv), Mexico (Central), and Middle East (UAE). Available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

This ensures that security measures, such as access controls, encryption, and logging, are applied uniformly, reducing the risk of unauthorized access, data breaches, and compliance violations. | Available in all commercial Regions. Available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

Available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

| [amplify-app-tagged](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/amplify-app-tagged.html) | ID.AM-02.01, ID.AM-05.01 | AWS Amplify apps are full-stack web and mobile application projects hosted on AWS Amplify. Enforcing this control helps ensure those aws amplify apps have tags. This is a detective check: it evaluates each resource and reports NON\_COMPLIANT when no tags are present, or when configured required tag keys are missing (tags beginning with `aws:` are excluded). Consistent tagging makes aws amplify apps identifiable in an automated asset inventory, supports ownership accountability and cost allocation, and enables attribute-based access control (ABAC) where authorization depends on matching resource and principal tags. Maintaining this configuration reduces security risk for AWS Amplify resources and supports consistent, auditable compliance. Without it, resources may drift from the intended secure state and go undetected. | Available in all commercial Regions except Africa (Cape Town), Asia Pacific (Hyderabad), Asia Pacific (Jakarta), Asia Pacific (Malaysia), Asia Pacific (Melbourne), Asia Pacific (New Zealand), Asia Pacific (Taipei), Asia Pacific (Thailand), Canada West (Calgary), Europe (Spain), Europe (Zurich), Israel (Tel Aviv), Mexico (Central), and Middle East (UAE). Each tag consists of a required tag key and an optional tag value. You can use tags to categorize resources by purpose, owner, environment, or other criteria. They can help you identify, organize, search for, and filter resources. They can also help you track resource owners for actions and notifications. You can also use tags to implement attribute-based access control (ABAC) as an authorization strategy. For more information about ABAC strategies, see Define permissions based on attributes with ABAC authorization in the IAM User Guide. The control fails if the API Gateway domain name isnt configured to use a recommended security policy. A security policy is a predefined combination of minimum TLS version and cipher suites offered by API Gateway. When your clients establish a TLS handshake to your API or custom domain name, the security policy enforces the TLS version and cipher suite accepted by API Gateway. Security policies protect your APIs and custom domain names from network security problems such as tampering and eavesdropping between a client and server. Using a recommended security policy helps ensure that API Gateway domain names use modern, secure TLS configurations that protect data in transit between clients and your API. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (New Zealand). | [appconfig-deployment-strategy-minimum-final-bake-time](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/appconfig-deployment-strategy-minimum-final-bake-time.html) | PR.PS-02.02 | AWS AppConfig deployment strategies use a final bake time to define how long the service monitors CloudWatch alarms after a configuration change has been fully rolled out, before declaring the deployment complete. During this window, AppConfig retains the ability to automatically roll back to the previous configuration version if an alarm fires. A bake time that is too short — or set to zero — closes this rollback window before application metrics, error rates, or latency anomalies have had sufficient time to surface, effectively disabling the primary automated recovery mechanism for bad configuration changes. This control enforces a minimum bake time threshold across all deployment strategies, ensuring that no strategy prematurely closes the observation window. Configuration changes carry the same risk profile as code deployments but are often subject to less scrutiny; a misconfigured feature flag, throttle limit, or connection string can cause cascading failures that only become visible under real traffic load. By requiring a meaningful bake time, this control ensures that automated rollback remains available during the period when impact is most likely to be detected. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Malaysia), Asia Pacific (New Zealand), Asia Pacific (Taipei), Asia Pacific (Thailand), and Mexico (Central). Not available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

| [appflow-flow-tagged](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/appflow-flow-tagged.html) | ID.AM-02.01, ID.AM-05.01 | A tag is a label that you assign to an AWS resource, and it consists of a key and an optional value. You can create tags to categorize resources by purpose, owner, environment, or other criteria. Tags can help you identify, organize, search for, and filter resources. Tagging also helps you track accountable resource owners for actions and notifications. When you use tagging, you can implement attribute-based access control (ABAC) as an authorization strategy, which defines permissions based on tags. You can attach tags to IAM entities (users or roles) and to AWS resources. You can create a single ABAC policy or a separate set of policies for your IAM principals. You can design these ABAC policies to allow operations when the principals tag matches the resource tag. For more information, see Define permissions based on attributes with ABAC authorization in the IAM User Guide. Dont add personally identifiable information (PII) or other confidential or sensitive information in tags. Tags are accessible to many AWS services, including AWS Billing. For more tagging best practices, see Best practices and strategies in the Tagging AWS Resources and Tag Editor User Guide. | Available only in US East (N. Virginia), US East (Ohio), US West (N. | [appmesh-virtual-node-service-backends-tls-enforced](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/appmesh-virtual-node-service-backends-tls-enforced.html) | PR.DS-02.01, PR.PS-01.06 | This control checks that ClientPolicy.Tls.Enforce is set to true for every entry in configuration.Spec.Backends[] on an AWS App Mesh virtual node. When this flag is false for any backend, the Envoy sidecar proxy is permitted to fall back to plaintext communication on that service-to-service path, even if the backend server advertises TLS support. Unlike the companion control APPMESH\_VIRTUAL\_NODE\_BACKEND\_DEFAULTS\_TLS\_ON, which governs the mesh-wide default client policy, this control targets per-backend client policies. A virtual node may have a compliant backend default while individual backend overrides silently disable TLS enforcement for specific service paths. This creates a gap where sensitive east-west traffic, that never leaves the mesh and may therefore be assumed to be encrypted, travels in plaintext between Envoy proxies. The consequence is exposure to on-path interception within the cluster or VPC network, undermining the confidentiality and integrity guarantees that a service mesh is expected to provide. It also breaks the assumption that mutual identity verification via TLS certificates is occurring on those paths, which is a prerequisite for meaningful service-to-service authentication. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Hyderabad), Asia Pacific (Malaysia), Asia Pacific (Melbourne), Asia Pacific (New Zealand), Asia Pacific (Taipei), Asia Pacific (Thailand), Canada West (Calgary), Europe (Spain), Europe (Zurich), Mexico (Central), and Middle East (UAE). This is a detective check: it evaluates AgentCore Memory and reports NON\_COMPLIANT when `EncryptionKeyArn` is not configured (i.e., a customer-managed key is not in use). Using a CMK renders stored agent memory unreadable without the key and places key policy and rotation under customer control, reducing the risk of unauthorized access to sensitive context data. The control also generates a FAILED finding for an AWS CloudFormation stack created by service-managed StackSets because there is no service role associated with it. Due to how service-managed StackSets authenticate, the roleARN field cannot be populated for these stacks. Using service roles with CloudFormation stacks helps implement least privilege access by separating permissions between the user who creates/updates stacks and the permissions needed by CloudFormation to create/update resources. This reduces the risk of privilege escalation and helps maintain security boundaries between different operational roles. It is not possible to remove a service role attached to a stack after the stack is created. Other users that have permissions to perform operations on this stack are able to use this role, regardless of whether those users have the iam:PassRole permission or not. If the role includes permissions that the user shouldnt have, you can unintentionally escalate a users permissions. Ensure that the role grants least privilege. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Hyderabad), Asia Pacific (Melbourne), Canada West (Calgary), Europe (Spain), Europe (Zurich), Israel (Tel Aviv), and Middle East (UAE). You can prevent a stack from being accidentally deleted by enabling termination protection on the stack. You can set termination protection on a stack with any status except DELETE\_IN\_PROGRESS or DELETE\_COMPLETE. Enabling or disabling termination protection on a stack passes the same choice on to any nested stacks belonging to that stack as well. You cant enable or disable termination protection directly on a nested stack. You cant directly delete a nested stack belonging with a stack that has termination protection enabled. If NESTED is displayed next to the stack name, the stack is a nested stack. You can only change termination protection on the root stack to which the nested stack belongs. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Hyderabad), Asia Pacific (Melbourne), Canada West (Calgary), Europe (Spain), Europe (Zurich), Israel (Tel Aviv), and Middle East (UAE). | [cloudtrail-event-data-store-multi-region](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/cloudtrail-event-data-store-multi-region.html) | DE.AE-03.01, PR.PS-04.02 | AWS CloudTrail Lake event data stores with MultiRegionEnabled set to false collect live events only from the single Region in which the data store was created. This creates blind spots in audit coverage: API calls, management events, and data events originating in other enabled Regions are silently omitted from the data store, leaving those activities unqueryable and potentially undetected. Enabling multi-region ingestion ensures that the event data store aggregates a complete, account-wide record of activity across all enabled AWS Regions into a single, immutable, SQL-queryable store. This is particularly critical for security investigations and incident response, where correlating activity across Regions — such as lateral movement, cross-region resource creation, or IAM actions that affect global services requires a unified event corpus. A single-region event data store cannot support this correlation. Additionally, global service events (e.g., from AWS IAM, AWS STS, and Amazon CloudFront) are recorded in specific Regions; without multi-region enabled, these events may not be captured at all depending on where the data store resides. From a compliance standpoint, this AWS Config rule detects event data stores where configuration MultiRegionEnabled is false and flags them as NON\_COMPLIANT, enabling automated, continuous enforcement of complete audit log coverage. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Malaysia), Asia Pacific (Melbourne), Asia Pacific (New Zealand), Asia Pacific (Taipei), Asia Pacific (Thailand), Canada West (Calgary), and Mexico (Central). | [codeartifact-repository-tagged](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/codeartifact-repository-tagged.html) | ID.AM-02.01, ID.AM-05.01 | AWS CodeArtifact repositories store and share software packages (e.g., npm, PyPI, Maven) for an organization's build and deployment pipelines. This is a detective check: it evaluates each resource and reports NON\_COMPLIANT when no tags are present, or when configured required tag keys are missing (tags beginning with `aws:` are excluded). Consistent tagging makes DHCP option sets identifiable in an automated asset inventory, supports ownership accountability and cost allocation, and enables attribute-based access control (ABAC) where authorization depends on matching resource and principal tags. Because the rule verifies the presence of tag keys and not their values, it evidences that resources are labeled — not that the label content is accurate. Maintaining this configuration reduces security risk for Amazon EC2 resources and supports consistent, auditable compliance. Without it, resources may drift from the intended secure state and go undetected. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Malaysia), Asia Pacific (New Zealand), Asia Pacific (Taipei), Asia Pacific (Thailand), and Mexico (Central). Not available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

With EBS encryption, you aren't required to build, maintain, and secure your own key management infrastructure. EBS encryption uses AWS KMS keys when creating encrypted volumes and snapshots. Enforcing this control helps ensure each repository is encrypted at rest with a customer-managed AWS KMS key (CMK) rather than AES256 or the AWS-managed default key ('aws/ecr'). This is a detective check: it evaluates each repository and reports NON\_COMPLIANT when a CMK is not in use. A CMK renders stored image data unreadable without the key and places key policy, grants, and rotation under customer control. Because the rule confirms a CMK is associated, not how keys are generated, rotated, or revoked. It evidences that encryption at rest is enabled, not that a key-lifecycle management program exists. Maintaining this configuration reduces data-at-rest risk for Amazon ECR resources and supports consistent, auditable compliance. Without it, repository contents may fall back to default encryption outside the organization's key governance and drift from the intended secure state undetected. | Available in all commercial Regions except Canada West (Calgary). | [ecs-task-definition-efs-encryption-enabled](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/ecs-task-definition-efs-encryption-enabled.html) | PR.DS-02.01, PR.PS-01.06 | Amazon ECS task definitions can mount Amazon EFS file systems as volumes. Enforcing this control helps ensure task definitions that use EFS volumes have in-transit (TLS) encryption enabled for the EFS mount. This is a detective check: it evaluates each ECS task definition and reports NON\_COMPLIANT when a task definition with EFS volumes does not have transit encryption enabled. Encrypting the ECS-to-EFS network path protects file data as it moves between the container task and the file system, reducing the risk of interception on the network. Maintaining this configuration reduces data-in-transit risk for Amazon ECS/EFS resources and supports consistent, auditable compliance. Without it, file traffic may traverse the network unencrypted and drift from the intended secure state undetected. | Available in all commercial Regions. | [ecs-task-definition-linux-user-non-root](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/ecs-task-definition-linux-user-non-root.html) | PR.AA-03.02, PR.AA-05.01, PR.AA-05.02, PR.PS-01.01 | When Linux containers run with root privileges, they pose several significant security risks. Root users have unrestricted access within the container. This elevated access increases the risk of container escape attacks, where an attacker could potentially break out of container isolation and access the underlying host system. If a container running as root is compromised, attackers may exploit this to access or modify host system resources, affecting other containers or the host itself. Furthermore, root access could enable privilege escalation attacks, allowing attackers to gain additional permissions beyond the containers intended scope. The user parameter in ECS task definitions can specify users in several formats, including username, user ID, username with group, or UID with group ID. Its important to be aware of these various formats when configuring task definitions to ensure no root access is inadvertently granted. Following the principle of least privilege, containers should run with the minimum required permissions using non-root users. This approach significantly reduces the potential attack surface and mitigates the impact of potential security breaches. This control only evaluates the container definitions in a task definition if the operatingSystemFamily is configured as LINUX or operatingSystemFamily is not configured in the task definition. The control will generate a FAILED finding for an evaluated task definition if any container definition in the task definition has user not configured or user configured as default root user. The default root users for LINUX containers are "root" and "0". | Available in all commercial Regions. Available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

| [groundstation-config-tagged](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/groundstation-config-tagged.html) | ID.AM-02.01, ID.AM-05.01 | AWS Ground Station config resources define reusable parameter sets (such as antenna downlink/uplink and tracking settings) referenced when scheduling satellite contacts. Enforcing this control helps ensure those config resources have tags. Consistent tagging makes config resources identifiable in an automated asset inventory, supports ownership accountability and cost allocation, and enables attribute-based access control (ABAC) where authorization depends on matching resource and principal tags. Maintaining this configuration reduces security risk for AWS Ground Station resources and supports consistent, auditable compliance. Without it, resources may drift from the intended secure state and go undetected. | Available only in US East (N. You can create tags to categorize resources by purpose, owner, environment, or other criteria. Tags can help you identify, organize, search for, and filter resources. Tagging also helps you track accountable resource owners for actions and notifications. When you use tagging, you can implement attribute-based access control (ABAC) as an authorization strategy, which defines permissions based on tags. You can attach tags to IAM entities (users or roles) and to AWS resources. You can create a single ABAC policy or a separate set of policies for your IAM principals. You can design these ABAC policies to allow operations when the principals tag matches the resource tag. For more information, see Define permissions based on attributes with ABAC authorization in the IAM User Guide. Dont add personally identifiable information (PII) or other confidential or sensitive information in tags. Tags are accessible to many AWS services, including AWS Billing. For more tagging best practices, see Best practices and strategies in the Tagging AWS Resources and Tag Editor User Guide. | Available only in US East (N. Consistent tagging makes provisioning templates identifiable in an automated asset inventory, supports ownership accountability and cost allocation, and enables attribute-based access control (ABAC) where authorization depends on matching resource and principal tags. Because the rule verifies the presence of tag keys and not their values, it evidences that resources are labeled — not that the label content is accurate. Maintaining this configuration reduces security risk for AWS IoT resources and supports consistent, auditable compliance. | [ivs-channel-tagged](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/ivs-channel-tagged.html) | ID.AM-02.01, ID.AM-05.01 | A tag is a label that you assign to an AWS resource, and it consists of a key and an optional value. You can create tags to categorize resources by purpose, owner, environment, or other criteria. Tags can help you identify, organize, search for, and filter resources. Tagging also helps you track accountable resource owners for actions and notifications. When you use tagging, you can implement attribute-based access control (ABAC) as an authorization strategy, which defines permissions based on tags. You can attach tags to IAM entities (users or roles) and to AWS resources. You can create a single ABAC policy or a separate set of policies for your IAM principals. You can design these ABAC policies to allow operations when the principals tag matches the resource tag. For more information, see Define permissions based on attributes with ABAC authorization in the IAM User Guide. Dont add personally identifiable information (PII) or other confidential or sensitive information in tags. Tags are accessible to many AWS services, including AWS Billing. For more tagging best practices, see Best practices and strategies in the Tagging AWS Resources and Tag Editor User Guide. | Available only in US East (N. | [lightsail-bucket-allow-public-overrides-disabled](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/lightsail-bucket-allow-public-overrides-disabled.html) | PR.DS-01.01, PR.PS-01.01 | Amazon Lightsail buckets support a two-layer access model: a bucket-level getObject setting that governs default object visibility, and an allowPublicOverrides flag that, when enabled, permits individual objects to be made publicly readable via public-read ACLs regardless of the bucket-level setting. When allowPublicOverrides is set to true on a bucket whose getObject is configured as private, any object within that bucket can be individually exposed to unauthenticated internet access through the S3 PutObjectAcl API, without changing the bucket-level configuration. This creates a condition where a bucket may appear private at the bucket level while containing publicly accessible objects, making the exposure difficult to detect through bucket-level inspection alone. Disabling allowPublicOverrides ensures that the bucket-level access policy is the authoritative and sole control over object visibility, preventing per-object ACLs from silently widening the access boundary. This control is most effective when applied alongside LIGHTSAIL\_BUCKET\_GET\_OBJECT\_PRIVATE, which addresses the separate condition where the bucket-level getObject is itself set to public, a state in which allowPublicOverrides has no additional effect. | Available only in US East (N. | [route53-recovery-control-cluster-tagged](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/route53-recovery-control-cluster-tagged.html) | ID.AM-02.01, ID.AM-05.01 | Tagging Route 53 Recovery Control clusters enables inventory tracking, ownership attribution, and criticality classification of disaster recovery infrastructure. A cluster provides the highly available control plane (five redundant Regional endpoints) that hosts routing controls for application failover. | [route53-recovery-readiness-cell-tagged](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/route53-recovery-readiness-cell-tagged.html) | ID.AM-02.01, ID.AM-05.01 | Amazon Route 53 Application Recovery Controller Recovery Readiness cells are the logical groupings of resources whose readiness for failover is continuously audited. Enforcing this control helps ensure those Route 53 Recovery Readiness cells have tags. This is a detective check: it evaluates each resource and reports NON\_COMPLIANT when no tags are present, or when configured required tag keys are missing (tags beginning with `aws:` are excluded). Consistent tagging makes Route 53 Recovery Readiness cells identifiable in an automated asset inventory, supports ownership accountability and cost allocation, and enables attribute-based access control (ABAC) where authorization depends on matching resource and principal tags. Maintaining this configuration reduces security risk for Amazon Route 53 Application Recovery Controller resources and supports consistent, auditable compliance. Without it, resources may drift from the intended secure state and go undetected. | Available only in US West (Oregon). Not available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

Without tags, organizations cannot identify which team owns each readiness check, what application it monitors, or what criticality tier applies, making it difficult to verify DR monitoring coverage and demonstrate recovery governance. | Available only in US West (Oregon). | [s3express-dir-bucket-lifecycle-rules-check](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/s3express-dir-bucket-lifecycle-rules-check.html) | ID.AM-08.03, ID.AM-08.05 | Amazon S3 Express One Zone, accessed exclusively through directory buckets, is a high-performance storage class optimized for latency-sensitive and compute-intensive workloads. Its elevated cost relative to general purpose S3 storage makes uncontrolled object accumulation a significant cost risk. Unlike general purpose buckets, directory buckets support only expiration-based lifecycle actions — objects cannot be transitioned to lower-cost storage classes. This means that deletion is the only available mechanism for managing storage growth over time. Lifecycle rules for directory buckets serve two distinct cost control purposes: expiring objects that have exceeded their useful age, and aborting incomplete multipart uploads that would otherwise continue to consume storage without ever producing a usable object. Without active lifecycle rules, both categories of data accumulate indefinitely, incurring ongoing charges with no operational benefit. This control ensures that every directory bucket has an active lifecycle configuration, establishing a defined retention boundary appropriate to the transient, high-throughput nature of the workloads these buckets are designed to support. | Available only in US East (N. Virginia), US East (Ohio), US West (Oregon), Asia Pacific (Mumbai), Asia Pacific (Tokyo), Europe (Ireland), and Europe (Stockholm). Not available in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions. |

| [ses-sending-tls-required](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/config/latest/developerguide/ses-sending-tls-required.html) | PR.DS-02.01, PR.PS-01.06 | By default, Amazon SES uses opportunistic TLS, which means emails can be sent unencrypted if a TLS connection cannot be established with the receiving mail server. Enforcing TLS for email sending ensures that messages are only delivered when a secure encrypted connection can be established. This helps protect the confidentiality and integrity of email content during transmission between Amazon SES and the recipients mail server. If a secure TLS connection cannot be established, the message will not be delivered, preventing potential exposure of sensitive information. While TLS 1.3 is the default delivery method for Amazon SES, without enforcing TLS requirement through configuration sets, messages could potentially be delivered in plaintext if a TLS connection fails. To pass this control, you must configure the TLS Policy to REQUIRE in your SES configuration sets delivery options. When TLS is required, messages are only delivered if a TLS connection can be established with the receiving mail server. | Available in all commercial Regions except Asia Pacific (Hong Kong), Asia Pacific (Hyderabad), Asia Pacific (Melbourne), Asia Pacific (New Zealand), Asia Pacific (Taipei), Asia Pacific (Thailand), Canada West (Calgary), Europe (Spain), Europe (Zurich), Mexico (Central), and Middle East (UAE).
